1	# STATUS
2	
3	Project: classify every public **musl libc** function into the formal-verification
4	partition it belongs to, with its canonical header and implementation source.
5	
6	## Deliverables
7	
8	| File                  | Purpose                                                          |
9	|-----------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------|
10	| `libc_functions.csv`  | the classification — one row per public function                |
11	| `classify.py`         | the generator (deterministic; re-run to regenerate the CSV)     |
12	| `test_classify.py`    | invariant + known-answer tests                                  |
13	| `PROGRESS.md`         | log of what has been done                                       |
14	| `STATUS.md`           | this file — current state and what remains                      |
15	
16	`musl/` is the upstream source tree (git-ignored; not part of this repo's history).
17	
18	## CSV schema
19	
20	`function,category,header,source,depends_on,syscalls`
21	
22	- **function** — public symbol name (one row per public symbol).
23	- **category** — one of: `pure`, `math`, `alloc`, `sys`, `io`, `pthread_core`,
24	  `pthread_sys`.
25	- **header** — canonical public include header (path relative to `musl/include`).
26	- **source** — implementation file (path relative to `musl/`).
27	- **depends_on** — `:`-separated list of functions this one calls. Every syscall
28	  primitive (`syscall`, `syscall_cp`, `__syscall*`, `socketcall*`, `sys_*_cp`, …)
29	  is normalised to the single pseudo-function `__syscall`. Only cross-file edges
30	  are listed (intra-translation-unit helper calls are omitted).
31	- **syscalls** — `:`-separated set of kernel syscalls the function reaches,
32	  transitively, over the call graph (target arch = **riscv64**; names use the
33	  canonical musl spelling, e.g. `openat`, `ppoll`, `wait4`). Computed with the
34	  same barrier rule as the category: syscalls never propagate *into* a function
35	  across a foreign `math`/`alloc`/`io` boundary, so `malloc` reports `mmap`/`brk`
36	  and `printf` reports `write`, but `strdup` (a pure consumer of the allocator)
37	  reports nothing. Consequently the column is **always empty for `pure`,
38	  `math` and `pthread_core`** — a built-in cross-check on the partition.
39	
40	## Categories (partitions)
41	
42	| Category       | Meaning                                                               |
43	|----------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------------|
44	| `pure`         | libc-pure — memory-only, assumption-free.                             |
45	| `math`         | libm — real + complex + `fenv`.                                      |
46	| `alloc`        | libc-alloc — the allocator as a resource provider.                   |
47	| `sys`          | libc-sys — thin boundary wrappers + errno; sole owner of ecall/MMIO. |
48	| `io`           | libc-io — buffered stdio; sole consumer of the stream model.         |
49	| `pthread_core` | thread/sync primitives that touch only memory/atomics (no syscall).  |
50	| `pthread_sys`  | thread/sync primitives that reach a syscall (futex, clone, …).       |
51	
52	## Current counts (1477 public functions)
53	
54	```
55	pure          431
56	math          280
57	alloc           9
58	sys           456
59	io            157
60	pthread_core   77
61	pthread_sys    67
62	```
63	
64	234 distinct riscv64 syscalls are referenced across the classified functions.
65	
66	## Method (how a function lands in a category)
67	
68	1. **Public symbol set** — parse function declarations from `musl/include/**.h`.
69	2. **Symbol → source** — musl's one-function-per-file convention, augmented by
70	   `weak_alias`/`strong_alias` targets and a real-definition scan; the file with
71	   the strong external definition wins on basename collisions (e.g. `clone`).
72	3. **sys-taint** — a static call graph is built from call-position identifiers
73	   resolved to the

### 🛠️ **Tool result**  ·  19:32:15; tool 8ms

```
1	# PROGRESS
2	
3	Reverse-chronological log of work on the musl libc partition classification.
4	
5	## Done
6	
7	- **Scaffolding** — git repo; `musl/` upstream tree git-ignored.
8	- **Classifier v1** — directory-based mapping of public functions to categories.
9	- **Taint-based classifier** — replaced directory heuristics with a static call
10	  graph + syscall-reachability ("sys-taint"), with `math`/`alloc`/`io` as
11	  non-propagating barriers. Fixed, in order:
12	  - call edges restricted to call-position identifiers (killed false edges from
13	    `#include <signal.h>` → `signal.c`);
14	  - multi-function files + internal helpers mapped via a definition scan
15	    (`err.c` → `warn`, `__tz.c` → `__secs_to_zone`);
16	  - syscall detection made macro-aware by parsing every `syscall.h`
17	    convenience macro (`syscall_cp`, `socketcall_cp`, `sys_pause_cp`, …) — the
18	    original regex missed `syscall_cp(`, mislabelling `read`/`write`/`poll` pure;
19	  - asm-implemented syscalls (`clone`) seeded from the riscv `ecall` mnemonic;
20	  - preprocessor lines stripped so `#define MAX(...)`/`MIN` stop forging edges;
21	  - **static functions made file-local** — musl's identically-named
22	    `static void dummy(){}` weak-stubs (in ~22 files) had cross-linked unrelated
23	    translation units, mislabelling `setenv`/`putenv`/`at_quick_exit` as `sys`;
24	  - basename collisions resolved by preferring the strong external definition
25	    (`clone` now → `src/linux/clone.c`, not the `src/thread` `-ENOSYS` stub).
26	- **pthread split** — thread/sync APIs separated into `pthread_core` (memory/
27	  atomics only) vs `pthread_sys` (reach a syscall), keyed on the API name prefix
28	  so `clone` stays `sys` and `pthread_*affinity_np` (in `src/sched`) are caught.
29	- **`depends_on` column** — `:`-separated cross-file callees per function, with
30	  all syscall primitives normalised to `__syscall`.
31	- **`syscalls` column** — transitive (barrier-respecting) set of riscv64 kernel
32	  syscalls each function reaches. Required, in order:
33	  - resolving the syscall-number convenience macros, incl. the `__`-prefixed
34	    internal forms (`__sys_open_cp` → `openat`, `__socketcall(socket,…)` →
35	    `socket`, `sys_pause_cp` → `ppoll`, `sys_wait4_cp` → `wait4`);
36	  - an arch-alias-aware riscv syscall set (`SYS_pread`→`pread64`,
37	    `SYS_getdents`→`getdents64`, … via `src/internal/syscall.h`);
38	  - excluding `__syscall_ret` (errno helper, not a syscall) — which also moved
39	    musl's `-ENOSYS` stubs (`brk`, `sched_setscheduler`, …) from `sys` to `pure`;
40	  - re-anchoring `DEF_RE` to column 0 so an indented `if (waitpid(...)) {` is no
41	    longer parsed as a *definition* of `waitpid` (had hijacked its source file);
42	  - excluding syscall primitives from `defsym` so a stray match
43	    (`syscall_cp` → `msgrcv.c`) can't forge edges (had polluted every wrapper).
44	- **Tests** — 17 invariant + known-answer tests (`test_classify.py`), all green;
45	  includes a determinism check (re-running `classify.py` is byte-identical).
46	
47	## Result
48	
49	1477 public functions classified:
50	`pure 431 · math 280 · alloc 9 · sys 456 · io 157 · pthread_core 77 · pthread_sys 67`.
51	
52	## Verification done along the way
53	
54	- Spot-checked ~60 functions with known-correct categories (all pass).
55	- Audited `sys`-in-pure-dirs and `pure`-in-syscall-dirs to catch both
56	  over- and under-tainting; every flagged case traced to a real cause and fixed.
57	- Confirmed no `pure` function carries a direct `__syscall` dependency, and no
58	  direct-`__syscall` caller is `pure`/`pthread_core`/`math` (enforced by tests).
59	
```

### 🛠️ **Tool result**  ·  19:32:15; tool 13ms

```
1	# Formally verified software for cyber security in the age of capable AI
2	
3	*We use language models to formally verify software end to end -- proof statement, planning,
4	and proof writing -- and we have a working verified artifact to show for it. As AI makes
5	attackers faster and proofs cheaper, formal verification finally pays off. This is a
6	proposal to push that frontier, with a concrete cost and a concrete deliverable.*
7	
8	## Motivation
9	
10	With the release of Mythos and the volume of new CVEs it has found, it is clear that AI's
11	impact on cyber security will only grow.[mythos-0] [mythos-1] [mythos-2] Even with
12	significantly less capable models, foreign actors have already successfully attacked parts
13	of the US's IT infrastructure.[oai-hack]
14	
15	One of the most robust defenses against software vulnerabilities is formal verification --
16	mathematically proving that code meets its specification. Historically, it was adopted
17	rarely, and for a clear economic reason: formal verification costs roughly **20 lines of
18	proof for every line of source**. The payoff was also low -- attackers discovered
19	vulnerabilities slowly, so the expected loss from any single unverified bug was small.
20	
21	Both halves of that calculus have now flipped. AI makes attackers far faster at finding
22	bugs, which **raises the payoff** of being verified. And AI makes proofs far cheaper to
23	write, which **lowers the cost** of getting there: the proof labor that once required
24	scarce specialists is increasingly automatable by language models. With costs falling and
25	stakes rising, we expect formal verification to move from a rare luxury to a practical tool
26	for robustness.
27	
28	## Previous work
29	
30	- [CakeML](https://github.com/CakeML/cakeml) -- a formally verified implementation of an ML-family language
31	- [CompCert](https://github.com/AbsInt/CompCert/) -- a verified C compiler
32	- [Frama-C](https://frama-c.com/) -- an open-source, extensible platform for source-code analysis of C software
33	- [seL4](https://github.com/seL4/seL4) -- a formally verified microkernel
34	
35	## Current work
36	
37	[This repository][m1el-fv] demonstrates that automatic formal verification -- proof
38	statement, planning, and proof writing -- works end to end using the Claude Opus 4.8 model.
39	We verified a bottom-up bootstrap tower (hex0 and hex1) across both Lean and Coq, with the
40	full work recorded as agent sessions for a transparent cost breakdown.
41	
42	We also [explored][m1el-fv-repro] whether open frontier models (DeepSeek4-Pro, Minimax-M3,
43	Kimi-k2.7) can reproduce the hex0 verification. The result locates the capability frontier
44	precisely: every model reproduces the *engineering*, and with a head-start can even *plan*
45	the proof -- but none completes the proof *execution* of the key loop-simulation lemma. The
46	wall is raw proof-engineering execution under kernel elaboration, not comprehension.
47	
48	## Proposal
49	
50	The proposal is multifold, all centered on improving cyber security -- a national-security
51	priority as of 2026.[us-cyber-fact-sheet]
52
```

```
=== a representative musl pure fn: strlen ===
#include <string.h>
#include <stdint.h>
#include <limits.h>

#define ALIGN (sizeof(size_t))
#define ONES ((size_t)-1/UCHAR_MAX)
#define HIGHS (ONES * (UCHAR_MAX/2+1))
#define HASZERO(x) ((x)-ONES & ~(x) & HIGHS)

size_t strlen(const char *s)
{
	const char *a = s;
#ifdef __GNUC__
	typedef size_t __attribute__((__may_alias__)) word;
	const word *w;
	for (; (uintptr_t)s % ALIGN; s++) if (!*s) return s-a;
	for (w = (const void *)s; !HASZERO(*w); w++);
	s = (const void *)w;
#endif
	for (; *s; s++);
	return s-a;
}
```

```
===== src/string/memset.c (90 lines) =====
#include <string.h>
#include <stdint.h>

void *memset(void *dest, int c, size_t n)
{
	unsigned char *s = dest;
	size_t k;

	/* Fill head and tail with minimal branching. Each
	 * conditional ensures that all the subsequently used
	 * offsets are well-defined and in the dest region. */

	if (!n) return dest;
	s[0] = c;
	s[n-1] = c;
	if (n <= 2) return dest;
	s[1] = c;
	s[2] = c;
	s[n-2] = c;
	s[n-3] = c;
	if (n <= 6) return dest;
	s[3] = c;
	s[n-4] = c;
	if (n <= 8) return dest;

	/* Advance pointer to align it at a 4-byte boundary,
	 * and truncate n to a multiple of 4. The previous code
	 * already took care of any head/tail that get cut off
	 * by the alignment. */

	k = -(uintptr_t)s & 3;
	s += k;
	n -= k;
	n &= -4;

#ifdef __GNUC__
	typedef uint32_t __attribute__((__may_alias__)) u32;
	typedef uint64_t __attribute__((__may_alias__)) u64;

	u32 c32 = ((u32)-1)/255 * (unsigned char)c;

	/* In preparation to copy 32 bytes at a time, aligned on
	 * an 8-byte bounary, fill head/tail up to 28 bytes each.
	 * As in the initial byte-based head/tail fill, each
	 * conditional below ensures that the subsequent offsets
	 * are valid (e.g. !(n<=24) implies n>=28). */

	*(u32 *)(s+0) = c32;
	*(u32 *)(s+n-4) = c32;
	if (n <= 8) return dest;
	*(u32 *)(s+4) = c32;
	*(u32 *)(s+8) = c32;
	*(u32 *)(s+n-12) = c32;
	*(u32 *)(s+n-8) = c32;
	if (n <= 24) return dest;
	*(u32 *)(s+12) = c32;
	*(u32 *)(s+16) = c32;
```

CakeML's standout finding: **Pancake** — a C-like imperative language with formal HOL4 semantics, a *verified* compiler down to riscv64, FFI, and Characteristic-Formulae separation logic. A real fourth option (write libc in Pancake, prove with CF, compile verified). But Pancake isn't C, and it's HOL4 (this repo is Lean+Coq).

POSIX findings: the function pages are consistently structured and mechanically extractable (DESCRIPTION/RETURN VALUE/ERRORS sections), 100% coverage of the pure string/mem targets, but POSIX prose defers low-level semantics to ISO C and leaves overlap/UB cases unspecified — usable as a spec *skeleton*, not a formal spec.

Waiting on the last and most directly-relevant agent: Frama-C.

```
1	/-
2	  A minimal, executable RV64I model -- exactly the 16 instruction encodings
3	  used by `bare/core.s` (hex0) and `bare/core1.s` (hex1):
4	
5	    ADDI ADD OR SLLI LBU SB BEQ BLT BGE BGEU JAL JALR   (hex0's 12)
6	    SUB SRLI LD SD                                       (added for hex1)
7	
8	  (li, mv collapse to ADDI; j = JAL x0; ret = JALR x0,ra,0; bgez/bltz are
9	  BGE/BLT vs x0). No LUI/AUIPC -- this is the whole trusted ISA surface for
10	  the hex0/hex1 rungs.
11	
12	  Words/addresses are `BitVec 64`, memory is byte-addressed (`BitVec 64 -> BitVec 8`),
13	  instructions are decoded from little-endian 32-bit words. The model is
14	  executable (`#eval`) so it can be run on the actual binary's bytes and checked
15	  against the QEMU run before any proof (see Hex0/Validate.lean).
16	-/
17	
18	namespace Rv64i
19	
20	abbrev Word := BitVec 64
21	abbrev Byte := BitVec 8
22	
23	structure State where
24	  reg : Nat → Word          -- x0..x31; x0 hardwired to 0 by rget/rset
25	  pc  : Word
26	  mem : Word → Byte
27	
28	/-- Read register `i` (x0 reads as 0). -/
29	@[inline] def State.rget (s : State) (i : Nat) : Word :=
30	  if i = 0 then 0 else s.reg i
31	
32	/-- Write register `i` (writes to x0 are ignored). -/
33	@[inline] def State.rset (s : State) (i : Nat) (v : Word) : State :=
34	  if i = 0 then s else { s with reg := fun j => if j = i then v else s.reg j }
35	
36	@[inline] def State.setPc (s : State) (p : Word) : State := { s with pc := p }
37	
38	@[inline] def State.loadByte (s : State) (a : Word) : Byte := s.mem a
39	
40	@[inline] def State.storeByte (s : State) (a : Word) (b : Byte) : State :=
41	  { s with mem := fun x => if x = a then b else s.mem x }
42	
43	/-- Fetch the little-endian 32-bit word at `pc`. -/
44	def fetch32 (s : State) : BitVec 32 :=
45	  let b0 := (s.mem s.pc).setWidth 32
46	  let b1 := (s.mem (s.pc + 1)).setWidth 32
47	  let b2 := (s.mem (s.pc + 2)).setWidth 32
48	  let b3 := (s.mem (s.pc + 3)).setWidth 32
49	  b0 ||| (b1 <<< 8) ||| (b2 <<< 16) ||| (b3 <<< 24)
50	
51	/-- Extract `len` bits starting at bit `lo` as a Nat. -/
52	@[inline] def field (w : BitVec 32) (lo len : Nat) : Nat :=
53	  (w.toNat >>> lo) &&& (2 ^ len - 1)
54	
55	inductive Instr where
56	  | addi (rd rs1 : Nat) (imm : BitVec 12)
57	  | add  (rd rs1 rs2 : Nat)
58	  | sub  (rd rs1 rs2 : Nat)
59	  | or   (rd rs1 rs2 : Nat)
60	  | slli (rd rs1 : Nat) (shamt : Nat)
61	  | srli (rd rs1 : Nat) (shamt : Nat)
62	  | lbu  (rd rs1 : Nat) (imm : BitVec 12)
63	  | ld   (rd rs1 : Nat) (imm : BitVec 12)
64	  | sb   (rs1 rs2 : Nat) (imm : BitVec 12)
65	  | sd   (rs1 rs2 : Nat) (imm : BitVec 12)
66	  | beq  (rs1 rs2 : Nat) (imm : BitVec 13)
67	  | blt  (rs1 rs2 : Nat) (imm : BitVec 13)
68	  | bge  (rs1 rs2 : Nat) (imm : BitVec 13)
69	  | bgeu (rs1 rs2 : Nat) (imm : BitVec 13)
70	  | jal  (rd : Nat) (imm : BitVec 21)
71	  | jalr (rd rs1 : Nat) (imm : BitVec 12)
72	  | unknown
73	deriving Repr, DecidableEq
74	
75	def decode (w : BitVec 32) : Instr :=
76	  let opcode := field w 0 7
77	  let rd     := field w 7 5
78	  let funct3 := field w 12 3
79	  let rs1    := field w 15 5
80	  let rs2    := field w 20 5
81	  let funct7 := field w 25 7
82	  let immI   : BitVec 12 := BitVec.ofNat 12 (field w 20 12)
83	  let shamt  := field w 20 6
84	  -- S-type immediate: [11:5]=funct7, [4:0]=rd
85	  let immS   : BitVec 12 := BitVec.ofNat 12 ((funct7 <<< 5) ||| rd)
86	  -- B-type immediate: [12]=b31 [11]=b7 [10:5]=b[30:25] [4:1]=b[11:8] [0]=0
87	  let immB   : BitVec 13 := BitVec.ofNat 13
88	      (((field w 31 1) <<< 12) ||| ((field w 7 1) <<< 11) |||
89	       ((field w 25 6) <<< 5)  ||| ((field w 8 4) <<< 1))
90	  -- J-type immediate: [20]=b31 [19:12]=b[19:12] [11]=b20 [10:1]=b[30:21] [0]=0
91	  let immJ   : BitVec 21 := BitVec.ofNat 21
92	      (((field w 31 1) <<< 20) ||| ((field w 12 8) <<< 12) |||
93	       ((field w 20 1) <<< 11) ||| ((field w 21 10) <<< 1))
94	  match opcode with
95	  | 0x13 => -- OP-IMM
96	      match funct3 with
97	      | 0x0 => .addi
```

### 🛠️ **Tool result**  ·  19:48:20; tool 2ms

```
1	/-
2	  hex0 functional specification (the refinement target).
3	
4	  This MIRRORS coq/Spec.v definition-for-definition. Bytes are modelled as
5	  `Nat` (assumed < 256); the input is a `List Nat`.
6	
7	  The spec is a two-state machine (EXPECT_HIGH / EXPECT_LOW), written as a
8	  single well-founded recursion `decodeS` over an explicit state `St`, so there
9	  is exactly one termination argument (decreasing on input length).
10	
11	  In LOW position the five "stop" characters (\n ' ' '_' '#' ';') yield `Split`;
12	  any other non-hex character yields `Unknown`; EOF yields `Trailing`. Capacity
13	  (OutputShort) is NOT part of `decode` -- it is applied separately in `coreSpec`.
14	-/
15	
16	namespace Hex0
17	
18	/-- Character codes used by hex0. -/
19	def c_nl   : Nat := 10   -- '\n'
20	def c_sp   : Nat := 32   -- ' '
21	def c_us   : Nat := 95   -- '_'
22	def c_hash : Nat := 35   -- '#'
23	def c_semi : Nat := 59   -- ';'
24	
25	def isSpace   (c : Nat) : Bool := c == c_nl || c == c_sp || c == c_us
26	def isComment (c : Nat) : Bool := c == c_hash || c == c_semi
27	/-- characters that, where a low nibble is expected, give `Split`. -/
28	def isLowStop (c : Nat) : Bool := isSpace c || isComment c
29	
30	/-- nibble value 0..15, or `none` if `c` is not an uppercase hex digit. -/
31	def nibble (c : Nat) : Option Nat :=
32	  if 48 ≤ c ∧ c ≤ 57 then some (c - 48)        -- '0'..'9'
33	  else if 65 ≤ c ∧ c ≤ 70 then some (c - 55)   -- 'A'..'F' -> 10..15
34	  else none
35	
36	/-- Terminal status of a decode (capacity-independent). -/
37	inductive Status where
38	  | Ok | Split | Trailing | Unknown
39	deriving DecidableEq, Repr
40	
41	/-- Drop characters up to and including the first '\n'. -/
42	def skipComment : List Nat → List Nat
43	  | [] => []
44	  | c :: rest => if c == c_nl then rest else skipComment rest
45	
46	theorem skipComment_len : ∀ l, (skipComment l).length ≤ l.length
47	  | [] => Nat.le_refl 0
48	  | c :: rest => by
49	      simp only [skipComment]
50	      split
51	      · exact Nat.le_succ_of_le (Nat.le_refl _)
52	      · exact Nat.le_succ_of_le (skipComment_len rest)
53	
54	/-- Decoder state. -/
55	inductive St where
56	  | High | Low (hi : Nat)
57	
58	def decodeS : St → List Nat → (List Nat × Status)
59	  | .High, [] => ([], .Ok)
60	  | .Low _, [] => ([], .Trailing)
61	  | .High, c :: rest =>
62	      if isComment c then decodeS .High (skipComment rest)
63	      else if isSpace c then decodeS .High rest
64	      else match nibble c with
65	           | none => ([], .Unknown)
66	           | some hi => decodeS (.Low hi) rest
67	  | .Low hi, c :: rest =>
68	      if isLowStop c then ([], .Split)
69	      else match nibble c with
70	           | none => ([], .Unknown)
71	           | some lo =>
72	               let (out, st) := decodeS .High rest
73	               ((hi * 16 + lo) :: out, st)
74	  termination_by _ l => l.length
75	  decreasing_by
76	    all_goals simp_wf
77	    · have h := skipComment_len rest; omega
78	    all_goals omega
79	
80	def decode (l : List Nat) : List Nat × Status := decodeS .High l
81	
82	/-- Numeric status codes, matching the Error enum in hex0.c / core.s. -/
83	def statusCode : Status → Nat
84	  | .Ok => 0 | .Split => 3 | .Trailing => 4 | .Unknown => 5
85	
86	/-- Behaviour of the bounded machine `core` with output capacity `cap`:
87	    if the decode would emit more than `cap` bytes, the machine stops with
88	    OutputShort (code 2) having written exactly the first `cap` bytes.
89	    Returns (status, output, out_len). -/
90	def coreSpec (input : List Nat) (cap : Nat) : Nat × List Nat × Nat :=
91	  let (bs, st) := decode input
92	  if cap < bs.length then (2, bs.take cap, cap)
93	  else (statusCode st, bs, bs.length)
94	
95	end Hex0
96	
```

## What I built

**(1) IL survey — CakeML, CompCert, Frama-C.** Two deep-dives into the actual semantics files converged on one finding:

> There's a sharp **structured→flat boundary** in every compiler stack, and it's exactly where proof difficulty flips. **Structured** ILs (CompCert **Cminor**, CakeML **stackLang/wordLang**, Pancake **loop/crepLang**) — `Seq`/`If`/`While`, no PC — are proved by *structural induction* (easy). **Flat** ILs (RTL→…→Asm, labLang, and this repo's `Rv64i`) — PC/CFG + label lookup — need *per-instruction PC-simulation* (hard). CompCert's RISC-V `Asm.v` is the same flat shape as your hand-rolled `Rv64i` — which is why `Refine.lean` is ~3400 lines. **Sweet spot = Cminor altitude.** (Frama-C has no compilation IL — it's CIL + a WP VC calculus, not a lowering stack.)

**(2) A new IL with verifiable compilation to RISC-V — `lean/LowIR.lean`, builds green.**
- `LowIR.Stmt`: three-address ops (1:1 with RV64I) + **structured** `ife`/`while`, no PC; big-step semantics `exec` (`LowIR.lean:90`).
- `compile : Stmt → List Rv64i.Instr` — lowers control flow to the **exact 16-instr trusted surface** (`bne`/`bltu` avoided by target-swapping) (`LowIR.lean:206`).
- `encode` — byte-exact inverse of the trusted `Rv64i.decode` (`LowIR.lean:265`).
- **Demonstrated on a real libc function, `strlen`** (written in LowIR), machine-checked end-to-end: `strlen_roundtrip` (emitted bytes decode back) and `strlen_rv_abc/_hello/_empty` — compiled `strlen` run as bytes by the genuine `Rv64i.step` returns 3/5/0. All 7 `#guard`/`theorem` pass.

Two write-ups: `docs/LIBC-FORMALIZE.md` §6 (survey + prototype + ladder steps 3–4), and the in-file T1/T2 target theorems.

## Two honest notes

- **I demoed with `strlen`, not full `hex0`.** Deliberate: `strlen` exercises the same constructs hex0 needs (`while`+`lbu`+branch+arith) at a fraction of the size, *and* it's a libc function — so it doubles as a libc-track demo. Expressing full hex0 in LowIR is the same machinery scaled up.
- **Checks are `native_decide` (certification-grade, finite — like `Certify.lean`).** The foundational payoff is the two general theorems stated in-file: **T1** = the compiler forward-simulation (proved *once* by structural induction — this is the `Refine.lean` cost, amortised into the compiler), and **T2** = per-program functional correctness at the easy structured altitude. `T1 ∘ T2` carries a structured proof down to real bytes.

## The existing designs (what's actually out there)

From the CakeML/CompCert semantics surveys, low-level ILs cluster into three control-flow regimes:

**1. Flat / PC machines** — CompCert `Asm` (incl. RISC-V), `Mach`, `Linear`; CakeML `labLang`; **and this repo's `Rv64i`.** State carries an explicit program counter (or a code-list "suffix"); control transfer is `goto label` / `branch → label`, resolved by scanning code for the label's position. Small-step.

**2. CFG of nodes** — CompCert `RTL` (one instruction per node, each naming its successor node) and `LTL` (basic blocks). State is `(node, regs, mem)`. Still small-step, still "where's the next node" reasoning.

**3. Structured register/stack ILs** — CompCert `Cminor`/`CminorSel`; CakeML `wordLang`/`stackLang`; Pancake `crepLang`/`loopLang`/`panLang`. Control is `Seq`/`If`/`While`/`Loop` with **no PC**; the program is a tree. Big-step (or both).

They differ along a handful of independent **design axes**:

| Axis | Range seen in the wild |
|---|---|
| Control flow | explicit PC (Asm/Mach/labLang) · CFG nodes (RTL/LTL) · **structured tree** (Cminor/stackLang/loopLang) |
| Semantics style | small-step relation · **big-step / clocked functional** (Cminor has both; CakeML is clocked-functional) |
| Variables | physical registers (Asm/Mach) · abstract locations+slots (Linear/LTL) · virtual-register map needing allocation (wordLang) · named locals with scoping (crepLang, Cminor) · shaped/typed values (panLang) |
| Expressions | three-address, one op per statement (stackLang, Mach) · **nested expression trees** (Cminor `x := a+mem[p]`, wordLang) |
| Memory | raw byte/word array · block+offset with provenance (CompCert `Memory.v`) · typed |
| Extras | continuation stack (CminorSel small-step) · liveness annotations (loopLang) · de Bruijn lets (CminorSel) · shapes (panLang) |

The decisive finding from the survey: the **structured↔flat** axis is the one that governs proof cost. Above it you prove by structural induction; below it you do per-instruction PC-simulation (the 3400-line `Refine.lean`). Everyone pays that cost *once* in a verified lowering pass — CompCert's `RTLgenproof`, CakeML's `stack_to_lab` — and reuses it.

## Why LowIR sits where it does

LowIR is engineered to be the **lowest *structured* altitude** — CakeML's "lowest still-structured IL is stackLang" / CompCert Cminor-minus-expressions — under four hard constraints specific to this repo: **target the existing trusted `Rv64i` (16 instrs), Lean-only, carry to native bytes, add as little TCB and compiler-proof burden as possible.** Each axis choice falls out of that:

| Axis | LowIR choice | Why |
|---|---|---|
| **Control flow** | structured `ife`/`while`, no PC | The whole point — buys structural-induction program proofs (T2), escapes the `Refine.lean` wall. |
| **Semantics** | big-step **clocked functional** (`exec : Nat → Stmt → St → Option St`) | Executable, so I validate-before-proving (`#guard`/`native_decide`, the repo's discipline) — that's how `strlen` was checked on real bytes today. Avoids the continuation-stack bookkeeping a small-step structured semantics (CminorSel) forces into proofs. Cost: threading fuel. |
| **Expressions** | **three-address**, one op per statement | Maps **1:1 to RV64I**, so `compile` per-op is near-identity and its correctness (T1) is trivial per op. Nested expression trees (Cminor/wordLang) would need temp-register management + an allocator *and its proof* — deferred to a higher IL layer. This is the single biggest reason LowIR is *below* Cminor. |
| **Variables** | physical register file, `x0=0` (mirrors `Rv64i`) | Operands compile to themselves — **no register allocation**, no allocation-correctness proof (wordLang's `word_to_stack` burden). |
| **Memory** | byte array `Word → Byte` (mirrors `Rv64i`) | Memory agreement in T1 is *identity* — no block/offset↔address translation. CompCert's block+offset model exists for C provenance; overkill for matching a flat byte machine. |
| **Conditions** | only `{eq, lt, ge, geu}` | Exactly the four trusted branches — **no new ISA surface** (the repo's ISA-surface discipline; the `bltu` exclusion is deliberate). `ne`/`ltu` are synthesized by branch-target swapping at compile time, not by adding instructions. |
| **Calls** | none yet | hex0/strlen are single functions. `Call`/`Return` brings a calling-convention + stack-frame layer (the Mach/Linear concern) and its proof — deferred. |

The unifying principle: **minimize both gaps at once.** Structured control minimizes the gap *up* to the program proof; three-address + physical registers + byte memory minimize the gap *down* to the machine, so the once-and-for-all compiler-simulation proof (T1) is as small as it can be while still buying structural program proofs. That's the optimal point for *this* repo — an existing flat `Rv64i` target and a minimal-TCB ethos.

**Alternatives I rejected:** reuse CakeML stackLang / Pancake loopLang (HOL4, not Lean; drags a whole verified compiler stack); port Cminor to Lean (Coq-derived, and its nested expressions force an allocator); stay flat and add assembler macros (keeps the `Refine.lean` wall); a CFG/RTL design (still per-node simulation). LowIR is the smallest thing that gets structural reasoning while compiling to the bytes you already trust.

Next, per your selection, I'll start on **hex0 in LowIR + proving hex0 + proving strlen** (which pulls in T1, the compiler simulation, as the shared foundation). Want me to begin with T1 (the reusable rung both proofs stand on), or land full hex0-in-LowIR executably first so there's a concrete target to prove against?

## What I added (`lean/LowIR.lean`, builds green)

The T1 simulation framework — the *relation* between an IL state and an RV64I state, and the theorem tying them through the compiler:

- **`Layout`** — where code is installed (`codeBase` + the `compile p` instruction list); `endPc` = fall-through PC; `inCode` = the code window.
- **`Installed L m`** — the code is faithfully in memory: the word at `codeBase + 4j` *decodes through the trusted `Rv64i.decode`* to `code[j]`.
- **`Agree L s m`** — all registers equal, and memory equal *off the code window* (the IL state has no code in its memory, so agreement only makes sense outside it).
- **`NoSelfModify`** — the separation side-condition (execution doesn't write into the code window), documented as a v1 approximation to be tightened when the proof is done.
- **`compile_sim`** — **T1 itself**, stated precisely: `exec … = some s'` + installed + agreeing ⇒ the trusted machine runs to `endPc` in a state still installed and agreeing with `s'`. Proof = `sorry`, with the structural-induction strategy written in the docstring.

```
/-- The whole decoder. -/
def hex0 : Stmt :=
  seqs
    [ lit 20 48, lit 21 57, lit 22 65, lit 23 70, lit 24 10, lit 25 32,
      lit 26 95, lit 27 35, lit 18 59, lit 19 255, lit 17 55, lit 16 1,
      lit 5 0, lit 6 0, lit 14 0,                  -- in_idx=0 out_idx=0 status=Ok
      .while .lt 5 11 body ]

/-! ### Harness: assemble + run on the trusted `Rv64i`, extract (status, out, len). -/

def codeBase : Word := 0x80000000
def inBase   : Word := 0x1000
def outBase  : Word := 0x4000

def memHex0 (code : List Byte) (inp : List Byte) : Word → Byte :=
  fun a =>
    let ca := (a - codeBase).toNat
    if ca < code.length then (code[ca]?).getD 0
    else
      let ia := (a - inBase).toNat
      if ia < inp.length then (inp[ia]?).getD 0
      else 0

def hex0State (inp : List Byte) (cap : Nat) : Rv64i.State :=
  { reg := fun i =>
      if i = 10 then inBase
      else if i = 11 then BitVec.ofNat 64 inp.length
      else if i = 12 then outBase
      else if i = 13 then BitVec.ofNat 64 cap
      else 0
    pc  := codeBase
```

### 🤖 **Assistant**  ·  21:01:12; model 43.7s

Here's the IL — `lean/LowIR.lean` — with the design rationale inline. Four pieces: syntax, machine state, semantics, compiler.

## 1. Syntax — three-address ops, structured control

```lean
abbrev Reg := Nat

/-- Branch/relational conditions — exactly the four available RV64I branches. -/
inductive Cond where
  | eq        -- ==          → beq
  | lt        -- <  signed   → blt
  | ge        -- >= signed   → bge
  | geu       -- >= unsigned → bgeu

/-- Structured near-assembly. Ops are three-address (map 1:1 to RV64I); control
    flow is structured (`ife`/`while`) with NO explicit program counter. -/
inductive Stmt where
  | skip
  | seq   (a b : Stmt)
  | addi  (rd rs : Reg) (imm : BitVec 12)        -- rd := rs + sext imm  (const via rs=0)
  | add   (rd rs1 rs2 : Reg)                     -- rd := rs1 + rs2
  | sub   (rd rs1 rs2 : Reg)
  | orr   (rd rs1 rs2 : Reg)
  | slli  (rd rs : Reg) (sh : Nat)               -- rd := rs <<< sh
  | srli  (rd rs : Reg) (sh : Nat)
  | lbu   (rd rs : Reg) (imm : BitVec 12)        -- rd := zext mem[rs + sext imm]
  | sb    (rbase rval : Reg) (imm : BitVec 12)   -- mem[rbase + sext imm] := low8 rval
  | ife   (c : Cond) (a b : Reg) (t e : Stmt)    -- if (a c b) then t else e
  | while (c : Cond) (a b : Reg) (body : Stmt)   -- while (a c b) do body
```

**Why this shape** — this is the deliberate part:

- **Structured control (`ife`/`while`, no PC).** This is the whole point. A program is a *tree*, so you prove it by structural induction (like `strlen_correct`), not by fuel-bounded PC-simulation against a decoded instruction stream (the ~3400-line wall in `Refine.lean`). The IL surveys (CompCert Cminor, CakeML stackLang/Pancake) all confirm: this is the boundary where proof cost flips.
- **Three-address ops, not nested expression trees.** Each op maps **1:1 to one RV64I instruction**, so the compiler is near-identity per op and its correctness (T1) is trivial per-op. Nested expressions (`x := a + mem[p]`) would force a register allocator *and its proof* — deferred to a higher IL layer. This makes LowIR the *lowest* structured altitude (CakeML's "lowest still-structured" is stackLang).
- **`Cond` is exactly the four RV64I branches.** No new trusted ISA surface. The deliberately-excluded `bne`/`bltu` are synthesized by branch-target swapping at compile time, not by adding instructions (respects the repo's ISA-surface discipline — the `bltu` exclusion is intentional).

## 2. Machine state — register file + byte memory

```lean
structure St where
  regs : Reg → Word          -- Word = BitVec 64
  mem  : Word → Byte         -- Byte = BitVec 8

@[inline] def St.rget (s : St) (i : Reg) : Word := if i = 0 then 0 else s.regs i
@[inline] def St.rset (s : St) (i : Reg) (v : Word) : St := ...   -- writes to x0 ignored
```

Mirrors `Rv64i` exactly: a register file with **x0 hardwired to 0**, over **byte-addressed memory**. Chosen so that (a) IL registers compile to themselves — no allocation — and (b) the memory-agreement relation in T1 is *identity*, not a block/offset translation. (CompCert's block+offset model exists for C provenance; overkill here for matching a flat byte machine.)

## 3. Semantics — clocked big-step

```lean
@[inline] def evalCond (c : Cond) (x y : Word) : Bool :=
  match c with
  | .eq => x = y | .lt => x.slt y | .ge => !x.slt y | .geu => !x.ult y

def exec : Nat → Stmt → St → Option St
  | 0,      _,    _ => none
  | fuel+1, stmt, s =>
    match stmt with
    | .skip            => some s
    | .seq a b         => (exec fuel a s).bind (exec fuel b)
    | .addi rd rs imm  => some (s.rset rd (s.rget rs + imm.signExtend 64))
    | .add  rd r1 r2   => some (s.rset rd (s.rget r1 + s.rget r2))
    ...
    | .lbu  rd rs imm  => let a := s.rget rs + imm.signExtend 64
                          some (s.rset rd ((s.loadByte a).setWidth 64))
    | .sb   rb rv imm  => let a := s.rget rb + imm.signExtend 64
                          some (s.storeByte a ((s.rget rv).setWidth 8))
    | .ife c a b t e   => if evalCond c (s.rget a) (s.rget b) then exec fuel t s else exec fuel e s
    | .while c a b body =>
        if evalCond c (s.rget a) (s.rget b)
        then (exec fuel body s).bind (exec fuel (.while c a b body))
        else some s
```

**Why big-step + fuel:** it's **executable** — that's how `strlen` and the hex0 battery were validated *before* proving (the repo's validate-first discipline). Every recursive call is at `fuel` from `fuel+1`, so it's structurally terminating *and* directly runnable (`#eval`/`native_decide`). The alternative — small-step with a continuation stack (CminorSel-style) — drags continuation bookkeeping into every proof. The cost we pay is threading fuel, but as `strlen_correct` showed, picking fuel exactly (`n+2`) makes the induction line up cleanly.

## 4. Compiler — structured → flat RV64I, cost paid once

```lean
def compile : Stmt → List Rv64i.Instr
  | .skip            => []
  | .seq a b         => compile a ++ compile b
  | .addi rd rs imm  => [.addi rd rs imm]          -- the ops: 1:1
  | .add  rd r1 r2   => [.add rd r1 r2]
  ...
  | .ife c a b t e   =>                            -- structured → positive branch + jal
      let cE := compile e; let cT := compile t
      condInstr c a b (4*(cE.length+2)) :: cE ++ jal0 (4*(cT.length+1)) :: cT
  | .while c a b body =>
      let cB := compile body
      let span := 4*(cB.length+2)
      condInstr c a b 8 :: jal0 span :: (cB ++ [jal0 (-span)])
```

The branch/`jal` **offset arithmetic** — the only place flat-PC reasoning appears — lives *here*, compiled once and reused by every program. `encode : Instr → BitVec 32` is the byte-exact inverse of the trusted `Rv64i.decode` (checked by `strlen_roundtrip`), so the output is real bytes the trusted machine reads back.

## The one principle

LowIR is engineered to **minimize both gaps at once**: structured control minimizes the gap *up* to the program proof (structural induction), while three-address ops + a physical register file + byte memory minimize the gap *down* to the machine (the compiler is near-identity). That makes the once-and-for-all simulation proof `compile_sim` (T1) as small as possible, while every program proof stays at the easy altitude — exactly what `strlen_correct` demonstrated and what `hex0_correct` is set up to follow.

Correct, and it's worth being precise about *what kind* of limitation this is — there are two different things going on.

## Computability vs. faithfulness

By the structured-program theorem (Böhm–Jacopini), `seq`/`if`/`while` over an unbounded state is already **Turing-complete**: any control flow can be re-expressed as a single `while` loop over an explicit *state variable* with a dispatch `switch` ("which block am I in?"). So LowIR can *compute* anything.

But that's the wrong question for two reasons:
1. **The emulation is a flat-PC machine in disguise.** A `switch`-on-a-state-variable inside a `while` is exactly a program counter and an instruction-dispatch — you've smuggled back the thing the structured altitude was supposed to buy you out of. The structural-induction advantage evaporates.
2. **It doesn't model the machine mechanism.** If the goal is to verify *existing* C that uses a computed `goto`, a function pointer, or `longjmp`, you must model *that mechanism* (an indirect jump, a saved context), not just a program that computes the same result. So "it's Turing-complete" doesn't get libc verified.

So yes — LowIR deliberately covers only **reducible, structured** control flow, and (so far) no calls. That's a real, intentional scoping choice for the proof-altitude experiment, not an oversight.

## What's missing, what it costs, where the cost lands

| Construct | Mechanism needed | Program-proof cost | Where the real cost lands | Precedent |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **break / continue / early-return** (multi-level) | forward jumps | low — extra outcome cases | semantics gets an `outcome` (`Normal/Break k/Return v`); stays big-step | CompCert `outcome`, CakeML `Break/Continue/Return` |
| **switch / jump table** | indexed indirect `jalr` through a table | low — case-split on scrutinee | **T1** (compiler must reason about the computed jump) | Cminor `Sexit`+table |
| **direct call / return** | calling convention + stack frame | medium — compose call specs | **T1** (frame invariants: save ra/sp) | Mach/Linear, Pancake `Call` |
| **indirect call / fn-pointers / vtables** | `jalr` to a *runtime* address | medium — "reg holds entry of fn satisfying S" | program *and* T1 (points-to-code predicate) | needed for `qsort`/`bsearch`/`dlsym` |
| **setjmp / longjmp, exceptions** | save+restore a captured context (sp, ra, callee-saved), non-local unwind | high — first-class continuation | both — must model the saved context | fv-libc flags these specially |
| **coroutines (stackful)** | switch between independent stacks | high | both — multiple explicit stacks | — |
| **irreducible CFG** (multi-entry loops, Duff's device) | — | n/a — not structurally expressible without node duplication / dispatch var | rare; usually restructure | — |

A couple of these I already hit: hex0's "early return" is faked with the `in_idx := in_len` trick (item 1, done crudely), and its character classification is a linear `ife`-chain standing in for a `switch` (item 2). Both work but are exactly the emulation tax — first real motivation to add the constructs properly.

## The honest architectural consequence

There's a fork, and a complete C stack needs **both prongs**:

- **Keep climbing structured** — add outcomes → calls → switch. This covers the *vast majority* of libc (our `pure` string/mem beachhead is plain loops + branches; structured is enough), and program proofs stay at the easy altitude.
- **A flat/CFG fallback underneath** for the genuinely non-structured minority — `longjmp`, stackful coroutines, computed `goto`, irreducible CFGs. These need indirect jumps / context switches, i.e. the flat-PC altitude we were avoiding. The structured IL then *compiles into* the flat one.

That layering isn't a workaround — it's exactly how the mature stacks are built: CompCert's **Cminor is structured** (`Sblock`/`Sexit`/`Sloop`) *plus* a `goto`/`label`, and it lowers to **RTL's flat CFG**; CakeML's structured `wordLang`/`stackLang` lower to flat `labLang`. The structured layer is where you *prove*; the flat layer is where the irreducible cases and the final machine live.

So the practical answer for the libc goal: add the constructs **in demand order** — break/return next (removes the hex0 hack), then direct calls (needed for any modular libc), then switch and function-pointers (needed for `qsort`/`printf`-family), and treat `setjmp`/`longjmp`/coroutines as a separate "needs an explicit-context / flat sub-IL" milestone. Each new construct's hard reasoning lands mostly in **T1** (the compiler), so program proofs keep their structural-induction ergonomics — which is the whole bet.

Good instinct — and the two are actually separable. n-ary `seq` is an *ergonomics* question; where break/continue point is a *semantics* question. Let me take them in turn and land on a concrete recommendation.

## n-ary seq: do it as a smart constructor, not a core change

You can get "blocks with many items" without touching the core inductive:

```lean
def block : List Stmt → Stmt := List.foldr .seq .skip
-- block [a, b, c]  ≡  seq a (seq b (seq c skip))
```

Why not make `Stmt` itself carry `block (xs : List Stmt)`? Because that's a **nested inductive** (`Stmt` containing `List Stmt`), and every proof by induction on `Stmt` then needs a companion list-induction and `sizeOf` bookkeeping — `exec` over a block becomes a short-circuiting `foldl`, and `strlen_loop`-style proofs get a second induction layer for free. Binary `seq` keeps recursion flat and structural (which is the whole point of the altitude). So: **n-ary at the surface, binary in the core.** You write `block [...]`, proofs see `seq`.

## break/continue: prefer option 2 (de Bruijn depth), over labels

Both your options work, but for *structured* multi-level exits, depth-indexed is the proven-clean choice — it's exactly what CompCert (`Sexit n`) and CakeML (`Break k`/`Continue k`) landed on. The mechanism is an **outcome** threaded through the (still big-step) semantics:

```lean
inductive Outcome | normal | brk (k : Nat) | cont (k : Nat) | ret   -- k = enclosing loops to cross

def exec : Nat → Stmt → St → Option (St × Outcome)
  ...
  | .seq a b =>                                  -- only run b if a finished normally
      match exec f a s with
      | some (s', .normal) => exec f b s'
      | other              => other              -- brk/cont/ret propagate, skipping b
  | .while c x y body =>
      if evalCond c (s.rget x) (s.rget y) then
        match exec f body s with
        | some (s', .normal)       => exec f (.while c x y body) s'   -- loop
        | some (s', .cont 0)       => exec f (.while c x y body) s'   -- continue THIS loop
        | some (s', .brk 0)        => some (s', .normal)              -- break THIS loop → exit
        | some (s', .cont (k+1))   => some (s', .cont k)              -- targets an outer loop
        | some (s', .brk  (k+1))   => some (s', .brk k)
        | other                    => other                          -- ret propagates all the way
      else some (s, .normal)
```

Each enclosing loop either **catches** index `0` or **decrements** `k+1 → k` and re-propagates. `break k`/`continue k` just inject `(s, brk k)` / `(s, cont k)`.

**Why depth beats labels for *this* job — and it's specifically a proof argument:**
- The de Bruijn arithmetic in proofs is a trivial `Nat` case-split (`0` vs `k+1`) — no environment, no decidable name-equality, no shadowing/freshness side-conditions. Named labels force the semantics to carry a label context and every `while`/block to do a *name match*, which becomes lookup reasoning in every proof.
- It composes with the structural induction we already have: `exec (seq a b)` and `exec (while …)` stay equational, you just gain a few outcome cases.

So: **option 2.** Reserve **named labels (your option 1)** for later — they're the right tool when you add *general* `goto`/`label` (the irreducible-CFG escape hatch from the last message), where there's no natural depth and readability/`goto`-target matters. CompCert does exactly this split: `Sexit n` (depth) for structured break, *and separately* `Slabel`/`Sgoto` (names) for goto. We'd mirror that.

One subtlety worth flagging: `break` and `continue` semantically target *loops*, so counting **enclosing loops** (above) is the clean index space. If you later want "break out of a labeled *block*" (jump to end of a `block` that isn't a loop — useful for error cascades), that's a separate `block`/`exitblock k` construct, again depth-indexed. Keep the two index spaces distinct rather than overloading one.

## What this buys hex0 immediately

It removes both emulation hacks I called out:
- The `err code := status := code; in_idx := in_len` trick (faking early return by poisoning the loop guard) becomes a real **`return`** outcome — `exec` stops, status is whatever's in x14, no guard tampering.
- The comment-skip and the nested error branches stop needing the "every error leaf must be the last statement" discipline — `break`/`return` make exits explicit instead of relying on `ife`-nesting to ensure nothing runs after an error.

And critically, the hard reasoning stays where we want it: program proofs gain a few outcome cases (still structural induction), while the compiler (T1) absorbs the lowering of `break k`/`continue k`/`return` to forward/back `jal`s — the cost paid once.

So let me retract the "separate index spaces" suggestion and answer the real question: **what actually bites is unifying the *index*, and only in one specific way.**

## The one genuine asymmetry: `continue` ≠ "leave a scope"

`break` and `block-exit` both mean *leave* a scope (jump to its end). `continue` means *restart a loop* — that's not leaving anything, and a block has nothing to restart. So:

- `exit`/`break`/`block-exit` → unify freely. One operation.
- `continue` → can only target a **loop**. It's the genuine exception; it can't be folded in.

If you fully unify into a single index that both `exit` and `continue` walk, then `continue k` can land on a *block*, which is ill-defined — you'd need a well-formedness side-condition "the k-th scope is a loop." That's a real bite, and it's avoidable by just not folding continue in.

## Where unifying `break` with block-exit bites: index fragility

Here's the concrete case. Suppose `exit k` counts **all** enclosing scopes (blocks + loops), CompCert-style:

```
while c {            -- loop   = scope 1 (from inside the block)
  block {            -- block  = scope 0
    ...
    break-the-loop   -- must be `exit 1`  (cross the block, then the loop)
    continue-the-loop -- `cont 0`  (continue ignores the block)
  }
}
```

Two bites fall out:
1. **`break`'s index now counts intervening blocks.** Wrap a chunk of loop body in a `block` and every "break this loop" inside silently needs `+1`. The index is no longer "which loop" but "how many scopes of any kind." Fine if a frontend computes indices mechanically (CompCert never hand-writes them); a footgun if the IR is hand-authored or readable — which ours currently is.
2. **`break` and `continue` of *the same loop* get different indices** (`exit 1` vs `cont 0`), because blocks count for exit but are transparent to continue. That asymmetry is inherent to unifying break with block-exit.

So the unification doesn't bite *soundness* — it bites *index stability and readability*, precisely because blocks then participate in the exit-index but not the continue-index.

## The clean resolution: make blocks transparent (no block-exit at all)

The way to make break and block-exit "not separate" without the bite is the other direction — **don't give blocks a catch at all.** A block is pure sequencing; only loops are exitable scopes. Then `break k` and `continue k` both count **loops**, symmetrically:

```lean
inductive Outcome | normal | brk (k : Nat) | cont (k : Nat) | ret

| .seq a b   => match exec f a s with | some (s',.normal) => exec f b s' | o => o   -- transparent
| .while c x y body =>
    if cond then match exec f body s with
      | some (s',.normal) | some (s',.cont 0) => loop again
      | some (s',.brk 0)                       => some (s',.normal)        -- catch
      | some (s',.brk (k+1))                   => some (s',.brk k)         -- decrement
      | some (s',.cont (k+1))                  => some (s',.cont k)
      | o                                      => o                       -- ret passes through
    else some (s,.normal)
```

`block` is just `foldr seq`, so it inherits the transparent `seq` rule — it catches nothing. Now:
- `break`/`continue` count loops only → **index stable under wrapping in blocks**, and **symmetric** (same loop, same index for both).
- There's simply no "exit a non-loop block early" — and in practice you rarely want it; you exit a loop you `break`, or you `return`. So break and block-exit aren't *separated*, they're *the same and the redundant one is dropped*.

If a real case ever demands bailing out of a non-loop block (some error cascade that isn't a loop and isn't a function boundary), *then* add an explicit catchable `block`/`exitblock k` — pay for it only when a target needs it.

So, bottom line: you're right that break and block-exit shouldn't be artificially split. The honest caveat is narrow — fully unifying the *index* makes `break` count intervening blocks and desync from `continue`. The cleanest fix isn't two index spaces (my earlier over-engineering), it's making blocks transparent so loops are the only thing break/continue count. That's the simplest core *and* the most refactor-stable, and proofs see one uniform loop-decrement rule.

Want me to implement this version — transparent `block`, `Outcome` with loop-indexed `brk`/`cont` plus `ret`, wire it through `exec`/`compile`, and convert hex0's error hack to `ret`?

### 🤖 **Assistant**  ·  21:39:20; model 1m35s

> 💭 (thinking)

It splits on what "break in blocks" means — and the two readings have opposite answers:

- **`break` *appearing inside* a block, targeting the enclosing loop** → **still works.** Blocks are transparent, so a `break 0` written inside a block passes through it to the loop. You don't lose this.
- **`break` *exiting* a (non-loop) block early** (the `do{…}while(0)` / error-cascade pattern) → **yes, B drops this.** If only loops are catchable, there's no block for `break` to target.

So if your "wish to have break in blocks" is the second one — early-exit a plain block — then loop-only break/continue **does** sacrifice it.

## Why you can't get everything at once (the actual structure)

There are three wishes, and with positional indices you can hold any two:

- **W1** break == block-exit (one construct, "don't separate them" — your stated preference)
- **W2** break can target a *block* (break out of a non-loop block)
- **W3** break and continue share one stable index

The conflict is forced by a single fact: **a block must count as a scope for *exit* but cannot count for *continue*** (you can't restart a block). So break (if it addresses blocks) sees blocks; continue never does. Therefore:

- **B (loop-only)** = drop **W2**. Symmetric, stable, simplest — no block-break.
- **A (unify break with block-exit)** = drop **W3**. One `brk k` = "leave the k-th enclosing scope, block or loop." This *is* your W1+W2. The price: `brk`'s index counts intervening blocks, so break-the-loop and continue-the-loop can have *different* indices when a block sits between them. `continue` is simply the lone construct that skips blocks — not a flaw, just the irreducible nature of "restart" vs "leave."
- **Named labels (your earlier option 1)** = drop positional indices entirely → you get W1+W2 *and* stable targeting, because `break 'blk` / `continue 'loop` name their target regardless of nesting. Cost: a label environment in the semantics.

## So, concretely

Your instinct (break == block-exit, and break works in blocks) is **design A**, and it's coherent — the only thing you give up is index symmetry with `continue`, which is unavoidable. That's the honest cost, and it's small: `brk k` leaves the k-th scope (block or loop), `cont k` counts only loops, done. Two outcome variants, blocks catch `brk 0`/decrement and pass `cont` through, loops catch both.

The *only* reason to prefer B (loop-only) is if you decide block-exit isn't worth the break/continue index desync — and given hex0's error cascade is exactly the block-exit pattern, I don't think it is; you want W2.

And if the "break counts intervening blocks" sensitivity later annoys you (it only bites hand-written IR, not machine-generated), that's the precise moment to reach for named labels — which is why your option 1 wasn't wrong, just premature.

My recommendation: go with **A** — unify `break` with block-exit (`brk k` leaves the k-th block-or-loop), keep `cont k` loop-only as the documented exception, add `ret`. It honors "don't separate them," gives you block-break for the error cascades, and the continue asymmetry is a one-line note rather than a real cost. Want me to implement that variant?

### 👤 **User**  ·  21:39:36; waited 509ms

How does CompCert approach this? CakeML?

### 🤖 **Assistant**  ·  21:40:56; model 1m45s

> 💭 (thinking)
> 

Two genuinely different philosophies — and they map cleanly onto the choices we've been weighing. (Summarizing CompCert from the source + the in-repo survey; I'll flag where I'm less sure of CakeML's exact spellings.)

## CompCert — one positional exit over *blocks*; break/continue/switch are *derived*

Cminor has no `break`/`continue` at all. The outcome type is just:

```coq
Inductive outcome := Out_normal | Out_exit (n: nat) | Out_return (v: option val).
```

and the scoping is `Sblock` + `Sexit n`, with the loop deliberately *transparent* to exit:

```coq
(* Sblock is the ONLY catcher — it decrements / catches Out_exit *)
outcome_block Out_normal      = Out_normal
outcome_block (Out_exit 0)    = Out_normal        (* caught here *)
outcome_block (Out_exit (S n))= Out_exit n        (* cross one block, decrement *)
outcome_block (Out_return v)  = Out_return v       (* passes through *)

(* Sloop: body normal → re-run; body anything-else → propagate unchanged *)
(* note: Sloop does NOT decrement Out_exit — loops are transparent to it *)
```

So `Sexit n` counts **blocks**, not loops, and a loop is escaped only by an `Sexit` that reaches a surrounding `Sblock`. The C front-end then *encodes* break/continue by wrapping each loop:

```
block {                 // outer
  loop {
    if (!cond) exit 0;  // exit outer block  ⇒  this is `break`-the-whole-loop
    block {             // inner
      body              // `continue` here = exit 0  (falls to loop end → re-iterates)
    }                   // `break`    here = exit 1  (crosses inner + outer block)
  }
}
```

So in CompCert: **block-exit *is* the primitive; `break`/`continue` are just `Sexit` at compiler-computed depths.** Even `Sswitch` is a *computed* `Sexit` — it evaluates the scrutinee and exits to the matching block (the jump table rides the exact same machinery). General `goto` is the one thing kept separate: named `Slabel`/`Sgoto`, resolved by `find_label`.

This is precisely **"design A"** from our discussion — unify exit, count all scopes (here, blocks), and accept that indices are positional. CompCert gets away with the index fragility because the indices are *machine-generated by the front-end*, never hand-written. Your instinct ("break == block-exit") is literally CompCert's foundation.

## CakeML / Pancake — nullary break/continue + exceptions for the rest

Pancake's source language goes the other way. `break`/`continue` are **nullary** — innermost `While` only, no index — and the result type carries them alongside an **exception** mechanism:

```
result = Error | TimeOut | Break | Continue | Return v | Exception eid v | FinalFFI
```

Multi-level / non-local escape (what CompCert does with `Sexit n` depth, and what we'd want for break-out-of-an-outer-block) is **not** done with indexed break — it's done with **`Raise`/handler exceptions**. So instead of "break 2", you `Raise` a typed exception and catch it at the right enclosing handler. (The lower IRs — `loopLang`/`wordLang`/`stackLang` — do carry *indexed* break/continue after the structured loops are flattened, per the in-repo survey; I'd want to re-check the exact constructor names against `third-party/cakeml` before quoting them, but the source-level story is nullary-break-plus-exceptions.)

So CakeML's answer to "multi-level break / break-out-of-block" is: **don't index break at all — use exceptions.**

## What it means for our question

| | scope counted | break/continue | block-exit | multi-level / non-local | goto |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **CompCert** | blocks (`Sexit n`); loops transparent | *derived* from `Sexit` | **primitive** (`Sexit`) | `Sexit n` (depth, frontend-computed) | separate named `Slabel`/`Sgoto` |
| **CakeML/Pancake** | innermost loop | **primitive, nullary** | — (none) | **exceptions** (`Raise`/handler) | — |

Both confirm the proof-ergonomic point from a few turns back: each threads an **outcome through a big-step/clocked semantics**, and each scoping construct catches / decrements / propagates — exactly the `exec : … → Option (St × Outcome)` shape I sketched.

For LowIR the two routes are:
- **CompCert route** = your "design A": one `exit n`, blocks are the catchable scope, derive break/continue, accept positional indices (compute them in a future front-end). Cleanest if LowIR becomes a compile *target*.
- **Pancake route**: nullary `break`/`continue` for the common case, and add an **exception/`raise`** outcome for the multi-level and error-cascade cases (hex0's cascade becomes a `raise`/handler, not an indexed break).

Notably, neither uses *indexed break that counts loops while block-exit is a separate thing* — i.e., nobody ships my earlier "two index spaces." CompCert unifies onto blocks; CakeML uses exceptions. That's a real signal: pick **A (unify, CompCert-style)** or **exceptions (Pancake-style)**, not the separated-spaces middle.

Want me to look at the actual `third-party/cakeml` and `third-party/CompCert` sources to pin the exact constructors and the `Sswitch`/`Sexit` encoding before we commit LowIR to one of these?

### 👤 **User**  ·  21:46:57; waited 5m35s

How would do {xxx} while (false); be encoded in A?

### 🤖 **Assistant**  ·  21:48:16; model 1m32s

> 💭 (thinking)
> 

Short answer: in A it's just **`block { xxx }`** — the breakable block. The `do/while(false)` idiom is the thing C programmers invented *because* C lacks a first-class breakable block; design A has one, so the idiom collapses onto the primitive.

Here's the reasoning.

## The mechanical (pre-optimization) lowering

The general `do body while(cond)` in A (CompCert-shape: `block`, infinite `loop`, `exit n` counts **blocks**, loops transparent) is:

```
block {                 -- B0  (break target)
  loop {
    block { xxx }       -- B1  (continue target)
    <test>              -- bottom test
  }
}
```

Indices from *inside* `xxx` (blocks innermost-first: B1, then B0; the loop is transparent):
- `break`    → `exit 1`   (cross B1, exit B0 → land after the whole thing)
- `continue` → `exit 0`   (exit B1 → land at `<test>`)

Now specialize `cond = false`. The bottom test becomes an **unconditional** exit of the loop. From the test's position the nearest block is B0 (depth 0), so:

```
<test> for while(false)   ≡   exit 0
```

## The collapse

The loop body now ends with an unconditional `exit 0` that escapes B0 — so the `loop` provably runs its body **exactly once** and never re-iterates. B1 (the continue target) and the `loop` are dead weight. It collapses to:

```
block {        -- the only surviving scope
  xxx          -- break → exit 0 ;  continue → exit 0 (one-shot ⇒ same effect)
}
```

`break` inside `xxx` → `exit 0` (leave the block → done). And `continue`, in a one-shot `while(false)`, semantically also just leaves (it goes to a test that immediately fails), so it *also* maps to `exit 0`. Both land after the block — exactly `do{xxx}while(false)` semantics: run once, any early exit jumps to the end.

## Why this is the interesting part

`do{xxx}while(false)` is precisely C's workaround for a missing language feature: **a block you can `break` out of**. Design A makes that a primitive (`block` + `exit 0`), so the idiom isn't an idiom anymore — you'd write `block { xxx }` directly, and that's literally hex0's error cascade flattened (`block { …; if err exit 0; …; if err2 exit 0; … }`).

And it pins down *why* A needs `block` as a separate construct from the loop: if the only catchable scope were a `while`, you couldn't encode this cleanly — you'd write `while(true){ xxx; break }`, but then a `continue` inside `xxx` would re-enter `while(true)` and spin **forever**, the opposite of do-while(false). You need a *non-iterating* catchable scope to make "continue also exits" fall out. That scope is `block`. So `do/while(0)` is the cleanest possible argument that block-exit deserves to be primitive — which is the whole "break == block-exit" position you started from.

### 🤖 **Assistant**  ·  21:52:39; model 1m38s

> 💭 (thinking)
> 

Good — that's a coherent, fully-specified design. To restate it crisply so we're aligned:

- **`block`** is the break scope; **`break-block k`** exits the k-th enclosing **block** (loops transparent to it).
- **`while`/`loop`** is the continue scope; **`continue-loop k`** restarts the k-th enclosing **loop** (blocks transparent to it).
- Two independent de Bruijn spaces; since LowIR is a compile target, the indices are machine-computed, so the "counts intervening scopes" fragility is a non-issue.
- **`break-loop` is unnecessary**: to exit a loop, wrap it in a `block` and `break-block` to that — the `break-block` crosses the (transparent) loop and lands after the block. Exactly CompCert.

## How `ret` works

`ret` is the *maximally non-local* exit: it leaves **every** enclosing scope and returns from the function. So in the outcome it's the one that **no `block`, `loop`, `seq`, or `ife` ever catches** — they all pass it straight through. It's caught only at the function boundary.

```lean
inductive Outcome | normal | brkB (k : Nat) | contL (k : Nat) | ret
```

The propagation rules (note `ret` is always the trivial pass-through case):

| construct | `normal` | `brkB 0` | `brkB (k+1)` | `contL k` | `ret` |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `seq a b` | run `b` | propagate | propagate | propagate | **propagate** |
| `block s` | `normal` | **catch → normal** | `brkB k` | pass through | **pass through** |
| `while/loop` | iterate | pass through | pass through | catch 0 → iterate / `contL k` | **pass through** |

So `ret` carries **no payload** — the return value lives in a register (RISC-V convention: result in x10/a0), so "return v" is just "put v in x10, then `ret`." And `ret` is *caught* at the function-execution boundary: today the top-level `exec funcBody s` returns its state when the outcome is `normal` **or** `ret` (both mean "function done"); once we add `call`, the call rule runs the callee body and treats `ret`/`normal` as "callee returned."

## How `ret` compiles

All three non-local exits are the *same shape* to the compiler — **a jump to a scope-boundary label**:

| IL outcome | target label |
|---|---|
| `break-block k` | end-label of the k-th enclosing **block** |
| `continue-loop k` | top-label of the k-th enclosing **loop** |
| **`ret`** | the function's single **epilogue label** |

This is exactly the point where the compiler grows past the current direct-offset fold into a **label + resolution pass** (CompCert's `Sexit`/`Sgoto` lowering, CakeML's `lab_to_target`): compile threading two label-stacks (block-end, loop-top) plus the one epilogue label, emit `jal`-to-label for each exit, then a second pass resolves labels to PC-relative offsets. The pure-`ife`/`while` compiler we have got away without labels because every jump was *local* (structural offset); non-local exits force labels.

Concretely for `ret`:
- **Today (no calls, leaf code):** the epilogue *is* the fall-through end of the compiled body (the current "halt" PC), so `ret` lowers to `jal x0, Δ` where Δ reaches the end label — a forward jump. The whole-program runner already stops there.
- **With a calling convention later:** the epilogue label points at the frame teardown (restore `sp`/callee-saved) followed by the real machine return `jalr x0, 0(ra)`. `ret` from anywhere jumps to that one epilogue.

And the payoff stays intact: the flat-PC reasoning for "this `jal` lands at the right scope-boundary PC" is discharged **once per construct inside T1** (the simulation invariant relates outcome `ret`/`brkB k`/`contL k` to the machine sitting at the matching label's PC). Program proofs keep threading the `Outcome` structurally — no PC, no offsets — exactly as `strlen_correct` already does for the `normal` case.

### 🤖 **Assistant**  ·  21:54:17; model 1m34s

> 💭 (thinking)

At the **machine level you're exactly right** — and I half-said it last turn: `ret`'s target label *is* the end of the function-body block (the epilogue), and `break-block`/`continue-loop`/`ret` are all "jump to a boundary label." So in the *compiler* there's no separate return mechanism; `ret` is the jump to the outermost (function) block's end, full stop. Unify them there.

The question is whether to also collapse it in the **IL/semantics** — i.e. drop the `ret` outcome and make `ret` literally `break-block N` to the function block. That's sound (wrap every body in a `block`, normal fall-through and `ret` both land at its end), but I'd keep `ret` as its own outcome, for three concrete reasons:

1. **`ret` is the one exit that's naturally *absolute*, not relative.** `break-block k`/`continue-loop k` are *relative* ("k scopes out"); `ret` always means "this whole function," independent of how deeply it's nested. Encoding it as `break-block N` forces the frontend to compute `N` = the block-depth *at every return site*. That's a bookkeeping obligation (and a bug class: wrong depth = jump to the wrong block) for precisely the exit that needs no index.

2. **The `call` boundary (coming next) wants to catch returns generically.** A dedicated `ret` outcome is caught by "run callee body, take its state on `normal`/`ret`" — no knowledge of the callee's block structure. If `ret` is `break-block N`, every function body must be wrapped in a block *and* every return's `N` must be correct relative to *that function's* nesting. You've pushed per-function structural invariants into the frontend to save one constructor.

3. **It's actually cheaper in proofs.** As its own outcome, `ret` is the trivial pass-through case in every rule (`ret → ret`, one line each). As `break-block N` it becomes the non-trivial index-decrement case at every `block`. Separate is *less* proof work, not more.

This is also why CompCert keeps `Out_return` distinct from `Out_exit n` — position-independence, generic matching at the call boundary, and (for them) carrying the return value.

So the clean split is the same one as before, applied here:
- **IL / semantics:** `ret` is its own *absolute* outcome — propagates through every `block`/`loop`/`seq`/`ife`, caught only at the function boundary. No index.
- **Compiler:** `ret` lowers to a jump to the epilogue label, which *is* the function-body block's end — sharing the exact label-resolution machinery as `break-block`. Your "exit to the outer block" intuition is precisely the lowering.

Net: unify in the lowering (one mechanism), keep distinct in the IL (an absolute return vs relative breaks). Full tree builds clean — 0 errors, all `LowIR.Ctrl.*` modules green. Here's the wrap-up of the control-flow work and the ergonomics finding you asked for.

## Implemented (5 commits)

| Module | What | Status |
|---|---|---|
| `LowIR/Ctrl.lean` | New IL: `block`/`while`/`brkB k`/`contL k`/`ret` + `Outcome`-threaded `exec`. 3 sanity `#guard`s | ✅ |
| `LowIR/CtrlStrlen.lean` | `strlen_correct` re-proved on the new IL | ✅ sorry-free |
| `LowIR/CtrlHex0.lean` | hex0 with **flat `ret` cascade**, validated vs `coreSpec` (17 cases) | ✅ `native_decide` |
| `LowIR/CtrlStrtoull.lean` | strtoull base-10: **break-out-of-loop** via `block`+`brkB`, validated (incl. 2⁶⁴ wraparound) | ✅ `native_decide` |
| `LowIR/CtrlStrtoullProof.lean` | break/block exec primitives (proved) + strtoull correctness scaffold | ✅ (1 deferred sorry) |

The design landed exactly as we agreed: `ret` is the absolute outcome (passes through everything, caught at the function boundary), `brkB` counts blocks, `contL` counts loops, break-out-of-loop = wrap in `block`.

## The ergonomics finding (your actual question)

**The control-flow machinery is nearly free to reason about.** Every new outcome rule is a one-line `by simp [exec]`:

```lean
exec_block_catch : block catches `brk 0` → `normal`        -- by simp [exec, h]
exec_while_brk   : loop passes `brk` straight through       -- by simp [exec, hc, hb]
exec_seq_brk     : seq short-circuits on `brk` (the cascade) -- by simp [exec, h]
exec_ret / exec_brkB / exec_seq_ret                          -- all one-liners
```

Concretely, per function:
- **strlen** — *no change*. It has no early exits, so the outcome is pure `.normal` plumbing; the proof is the same shape as before, marginally more verbose. Honest result: the control flow is neutral-to-slightly-negative for a single straight loop.
- **hex0** — *big win at the program level*. Error handling went from deeply-nested ifes + the `in_idx := in_len` poison-the-guard hack to a **flat** sequence of `ife guard (err) skip` where `err = set status; ret`. That flatness is exactly what makes the eventual proof flatter — each error path becomes an independent guard rather than a nesting level.
- **strtoull** — break-out-of-loop is clean (`block { while(true) { … brkB 0 … } }`), and in the proof the break is just `exec_while_brk` then `exec_block_catch` — two trivial steps.

So: the new constructs don't make the proof *mechanics* harder (the primitives are one-liners), and they make the *programs* substantially cleaner for anything with real control flow — which is the whole point, since libc is full of early returns and error cascades.

## Open items
- `strtoull10_correct` — stated with the `digit_loop` invariant fully documented (same shape as `strlen_loop` + `acc_times_ten` [proved] + the `brk 0` terminating iteration). Induction deferred (`sorry`), backed by the passing executable battery.
- Carryover sorries: `compile_sim` (T1) and `hex0_correct`.
- The **label-based compiler** for the new constructs (`block`/`brkB`/`contL`/`ret` → boundary-label jumps) is the remaining piece to take `LowIR.Ctrl` programs to real RISC-V bytes; I validated everything at the IL level instead this round.

```
# Status — verified hex0 on bare-metal RISC-V

Goal: run hex0 on bare-metal `qemu-system-riscv` **and** have a formal proof
(in both Lean and Coq) that the bytes that run implement the hex0 spec.

## What is done (and its strength)

| # | Deliverable | Strength |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | hex0 runs bare-metal on `qemu-system-riscv64 -M virt`; decodes embedded input to `Hello\n`, verified byte-for-byte | concrete, runs |
| 2 | Shared `decode` spec in **Lean + Coq**, computing identically on a battery | spec, cross-checked |
| 3 | Hand-rolled **executable RV64I** model in **Lean + Coq** (the 12 instr forms `core` uses) | model |
| 4 | Both models execute the **real binary** and match `coreSpec` (13-input differential battery, all error classes) | testing-grade, cross-validated vs QEMU |
| 5 | **Certification** theorems: deployed bytes = `coreSpec` on embedded input + battery. Lean via `native_decide`; **Coq via `vm_compute` (kernel-checked)** | **finite / testing-grade** (proved, but covers finitely many inputs) |
| 6 | **General refinement** (Lean **and Coq**): `core_refines : ∀ inp cap, WellFormed inp cap → ∃ fuel, observe inp cap fuel = coreSpec inp cap`, **fully proved**. Lean: `sorry`-free, `#print axioms` = `[propext, Quot.sound]` (`Classical.choice`-free). Coq: `Admitted`-free, `Print Assumptions` = `[functional_extensionality_dep]` only. | **proof-grade, COMPLETE (both)** |
| 7a | **ISA cross-check, decode half** (`coq/RvCross.v`): `decode_agrees : ∀ w, 0≤w<2³² → ∀ i, Rv64i.decode w = i → i ≠ unknown → Decode.decode RV64I w = embed i` — our decoder proved equal to **riscv-coq** (`coq-riscv.0.0.5`, the bedrock2/`compiler` reference model) on all 12 forms, for every 32-bit word. `Admitted`-free; `Print Assumptions` = **Closed under the global context (zero axioms)**. | **proof-grade, COMPLETE** |
| 7b | ISA cross-check, **execute/step half** (`step_agrees`, forward simulation vs riscv-coq's `Run.run1` over the Minimal `OState` machine) + transport corollary `core_refines_riscv` | **PROVED.** `step_agrees` (`coq/RvCrossExec.v`: 12 `exec_*` + dispatch on `decode (fetch32 s)`) and the transport corollary `core_refines_riscv` (`coq/RvCrossRun.v`: `transport_run` inducts `step_agrees` over a whole run via the `rrun` lockstep iterator, composed with `init_loopinv`+`loop_correct`) are both `Admitted`-free, `Print Assumptions` ⇒ only `functional_extensionality_dep`. Under `Rrel`+`WFstep` one `Rv64i.step` = one `run1 RV64I` cycle; the corollary lifts this so the riscv-coq reference model running the real `core` bytes computes `coreSpec`, modulo two **explicit** factored hypotheses (init-state `Rrel` match + per-step `RunWF` along the run). Reverse decode `decode_agrees_rev`+`embed_inj` (`coq/RvCross.v`, ZERO axioms) also added. Remaining: discharge the two corollary hypotheses unconditionally |
```

### 🤖 **Assistant**  ·  22:20:22; model 1m08s

> 💭 (thinking)
> 

Good catch — and the honest answer is that **it doesn't, and that's a real conformance gap I should flag.**

My current `strtoull10`/`strtoullSpec` use **silent modular wraparound** (BitVec-64 `acc*10 + digit`). The implementation and the reference spec agree with each other — that's all `strtoull10_matches_spec` checks — but they do **not** match the C standard:

- **C/POSIX strtoull on overflow:** returns `ULLONG_MAX` (2⁶⁴−1), sets `errno = ERANGE`, and *keeps consuming the remaining digits* so `endptr` still points past the whole number. It **saturates**, it doesn't wrap.
- **Mine:** wraps mod 2⁶⁴. So my battery's `"18446744073709551616"` case, which I labeled "wraparound → 0", is exactly where I diverge: real strtoull returns 2⁶⁴−1 + ERANGE there; mine returns 0. The test passes only because impl and spec are wrong *together*.

So the "base-10 core" caveat I wrote is hiding a genuine semantic shortfall, not just a missing convenience.

**What conformance requires** (per digit, before `acc := acc*10 + d`):
```
overflow  iff  acc > ULLONG_MAX/10            // 1844674407370955161
          or  (acc == ULLONG_MAX/10 and d > 5) // ULLONG_MAX%10 == 5
```
On overflow: set a saturate flag, fix `result := ULLONG_MAX`, set `ERANGE`, and **keep scanning** digits (don't break) until the first non-digit. This is what musl actually does.

Two wrinkles specific to our restricted ISA:
1. The threshold `1844674407370955161` is a 64-bit constant, but `addi` only carries a **12-bit** immediate — so conformant overflow detection needs a constant-construction sequence (shift/or) or an alternate check (e.g. detect that `acc*10` overflowed by comparing `acc` to `acc*10`'s high behavior). Worth noting it's not free in this ISA surface.
2. (Related gap, same spirit) strtoull also accepts a leading `-` and negates **mod 2⁶⁴** without erroring — another thing the core skips.

For the proof I'm mid-way through (item a): it's proving `impl ≡ spec` for the **wrapping** semantics — internally valid, but it's the wrong spec for real strtoull. So before I finish it, the decision is yours:

**(i)** keep the wrapping core, finish the proof as-is, and clearly mark it "non-conformant on overflow" (a stepping stone); or **(ii)** make the spec conformant first — add saturate + an `ERANGE`/flag output (and the constant-construction), update the impl, then prove *that*.

I'd lean **(ii)** since you're asking — overflow is the interesting part of strtoull and the whole point is faithfulness. Which do you want? (I also just noticed, while writing the proof, I referenced an `exec_add` lemma I haven't defined yet — trivial to add — so the build is mid-edit regardless.)
